\displaystyle[\Gamma,B(f,g)]=B^{\Gamma}(f,g)

with symbols

(A.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}b^{S}(f,g)&:=-(\xi\cdot\nabla_{\xi}+\eta\cdot\nabla_{\eta})b(\xi,\eta),\\
b^{\Omega}(f,g)&:=(\xi\wedge\nabla_{\xi}+\eta\wedge\nabla_{\eta})b(\xi,\eta),\end{split}

Proof.

These formulas can be checked by a direct calculation.
∎

Here is basic lemma to handle product and pseudo-product in our weighted spaces.

Lemma A.4
.

Recall the definition (
2.8
) with (
2.3
). For all
N\geq 0

(A.20)

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N}{\|fg\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r+2k\leq N/2}{\|f\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}}\sum_{r+k\leq N}{\|g\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}+\sum_{r+k\leq N}{\|f\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\sum_{r+k\leq N/2}{\|g\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}},

and, more in general, for
1/p=1/p_{1}+1/p_{2}=1/q_{1}+1/q_{2}

(A.21)

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N}{\|fg\|}_{Z^{r,p}_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r+k\leq N/2}{\|f\|}_{Z^{r,p_{1}}_{k}}\sum_{r+k\leq N}{\|g\|}_{Z^{r,p_{2}}_{k}}+\sum_{r+k\leq N}{\|f\|}_{Z^{r,q_{1}}_{k}}\sum_{r+k\leq N/2}{\|g\|}_{Z^{r,q_{2}}_{k}}.

The same bounds hold if we replace the product
fg
by a pseudo-product
B(f,g)
as in (
A.12
) with
a symbol
b
satisfying

(A.22)

\displaystyle{\big\|b^{\Gamma^{k}}\big\|}_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim 1,\quad k=0,\dots,N,

where
b^{\Gamma}
,
\Gamma\in\{S,\Omega\}
is defined as in (
A.19
) and

b^{\Gamma^{k}}
is defined inductively by
b^{\Gamma^{k}}=(b^{\Gamma^{k-1}})^{\Gamma}
with
b_{0}=b
.

Proof.

We use the notation from
4.2.1
for repeated applications of vector fields
(just
2
d ones, here).
For any
|r|+|k|\leq N
with
r=r_{1}+r_{2}
and
k=k_{1}+k_{2}
we have

(A.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{x}^{r}\Gamma^{k}(fg)&=\big(\nabla_{x}^{r_{1}}\Gamma^{k_{1}}f\big)\,\big(\nabla_{x}^{r_{2}}\Gamma^{k_{2}}g\big).\end{split}

Without loss of generality, by the symmetry of the right-hand side of (
A.20
),
we may assume that
|r_{1}|+|k_{1}|\leq N/2
and estimate

\displaystyle{\big\|\big(\nabla_{x}^{r_{1}}\Gamma^{k_{1}}f\big)\,\big(\nabla_{x}^{r_{2}}\Gamma^{k_{2}}g\big)\big\|}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\big\|\nabla_{x}^{r_{1}}\Gamma^{k_{1}}f\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}{\big\|\nabla_{x}^{r_{2}}\Gamma^{k_{2}}g\big\|}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r_{1}+k_{1}\leq N/2}{\big\|f\big\|}_{Z^{r_{1},\infty}_{k_{1}}}\sum_{r_{2}+k_{2}\leq N}{\big\|g\big\|}_{Z^{r_{2}}_{k_{2}}}.

The estimate (
A.21
) follows identically.
For the same estimate with
f\,g
replaced by
B(f,g)
it suffices to use Lemma
A.3

to commute vector fields, followed by an application (
A.16
) using the assumption (
A.22
).
∎

We also use the following standard product estimate:

Lemma A.5
.

for
f,g:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
, the following estimate holds:

(A.24)

\displaystyle{\big\||\nabla|^{1/2}(fg)\big\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\|f\|}_{W^{1,3}}{\big\||\nabla|^{1/2}g\big\|}_{L^{2}},

A.3.
A basic trace inequality

We use the following basic trace estimate:

Lemma A.6

(Trace inequalities)
.

Let
f:\mathcal{D}_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
, define

F:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(-\infty,0]
by
F(x,z)=f(x,z+h(x))
,
and assume that
\lim_{z\rightarrow-\infty}F=0
.
Then,

(A.25)

\displaystyle\||\nabla|^{1/2}F\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{x,z}F\|_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{z}},

(A.26)

\displaystyle\|F\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|F\|_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}+\|\nabla_{x,z}F\|_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{z}}.

In particular,
if
\nabla h\in L^{\infty}
,

(A.27)

\displaystyle\||\nabla|^{1/2}F\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{x,y}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D}_{t})},

(A.28)

\displaystyle\|F\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D}_{t})}+\|\nabla_{x,y}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D}_{t})}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}||\nabla|^{1/2}F(x,0)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=2\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{z}|\nabla|^{1/2}F(x,z)|\nabla|^{1/2}F(x,z)\,dxdz

\displaystyle=2\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{z}F(x,z)|\nabla|F(x,z)\,dxdz

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{x,z}F\|_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\lesssim(1+\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})^{2}\|\nabla_{x,y}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{D}_{t})}^{2},

which gives (
A.25
) and (
A.27
). The bounds
(
A.26
) and (
A.28
) are proved
in exactly the same way without including the factor
|\nabla|^{1/2}
.
∎

Appendix B
The boundary equations

Recall the definitions

(B.1)

\displaystyle v=\nabla\psi+v_{\omega},\qquad\varphi=\psi|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}

with
\Delta\psi=0
, and
v_{\omega}\cdot n=0
.
Define the restrictions of the horizontal and vertical components to the boundary as follows:

(B.2)

\displaystyle v|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}=(P,B),\qquad\nabla\psi|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}=(P_{ir},B_{ir}),\qquad v_{\omega}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}=\widetilde{v_{\omega}}=(P_{\omega},B_{\omega}).

We have

(B.3)

\displaystyle P_{ir}=\nabla\varphi-B_{ir}\nabla h,\qquad B_{ir}=\frac{G(h)\varphi+\nabla h\cdot\nabla\varphi}{1+|\nabla h|^{2}},

Recall that

(B.4)

\displaystyle G(h)\varphi=(P_{ir},B_{ir})\cdot(-\nabla h,1)=(P,B)\cdot(-\nabla h,1),

where the last identity follows since
v_{\omega}\cdot n=0
and, therefore, we have

(B.5)

\displaystyle B_{\omega}=\nabla h\cdot P_{\omega},

that is, the rotational part of the velocity does not move the boundary.
Moreover, one can show that there exists
a_{\omega}
such that

(B.6)

\displaystyle\nabla a_{\omega}=U_{\omega}:=P_{\omega}+\nabla hB_{\omega},

since
\partial_{2}U_{\omega}^{1}-\partial_{1}U_{\omega}^{2}=\omega|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\cdot(-\nabla h,1)=0

in view of our assumption that
\omega
vanishes on the boundary.

Notice that we can also express
a_{\omega}
just in terms of
h
and
P_{\omega}
:

(B.7)

\displaystyle a_{\omega}=\Lambda^{-1}R\cdot\nabla a_{\omega}=\Lambda^{-1}R\cdot\big(P_{\omega}+\nabla h(\nabla h\cdot P_{\omega})\big).

B.1.
Evolution equations at the boundary

The equation for the motion of the interface is the standard
\partial_{t}h=G(h)\varphi
.
The equation for the evolution of the potential is more involved and given by the following:

Lemma B.1

(Boundary evolution equations)
.

Assume that
\omega\cdot n=0
. With the notation (
B.1
)-(
B.6
) we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\partial_{t}h&=G(h)\varphi\\
\partial_{t}\varphi&=-h-\dfrac{1}{2}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}\dfrac{(G(h)\varphi+\nabla h\cdot\nabla\varphi)^{2}}{1+|\nabla h|^{2}}-\Lambda^{-1}R\cdot\partial_{t}P_{\omega}-\nabla\varphi\cdot P_{\omega}-\dfrac{1}{2}|P_{\omega}|^{2}+R_{\omega}\end{array}\right.

where

(B.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{\omega}=-\Lambda^{-1}R\cdot\partial_{t}\big(\nabla h(\nabla h\cdot P_{\omega})\big)-\frac{1}{2}(P_{\omega}\cdot\nabla h)^{2}+(G(h)\varphi)P_{\omega}\cdot\nabla h.\end{split}

Remark B.2
.

In the irrotational case the Zakharov formulation in terms of
(P,B)
reads

(B.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}\varphi&=-h-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(1+|\nabla h|^{2})B^{2}=-h-\frac{1}{2}P^{2}+\frac{1}{2}B^{2}-B\nabla h\cdot P.\end{split}

One can check (and we will do this in the proof below)
that the same equation extends to the rotational case, in the sense that

(B.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}(P+\nabla hB)&=\nabla\big(-h-\frac{1}{2}P^{2}+\frac{1}{2}B^{2}-B\nabla h\cdot P\big),\end{split}

where
(P,B)
are now defined as in (
B.2
).
Then, by expanding
P=P_{ir}+P_{\omega}
and
B=B_{ir}+B_{\omega}
, using (
B.3
) and (
B.5
),
(
B.6
) and (
B.7
), one can arrive at (
B.1
)-(
B.10
).

Equation (
B.12
) is also equivalent to the formulation used by Castro-Lannes
[
8
]

which has the form

(B.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}U_{\parallel}&=-\nabla h-\frac{1}{2}\nabla|U_{\parallel}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\big((1+|\nabla h|^{2})B^{2}\big)\end{split}

where
U_{\parallel}=P+B\nabla h=\nabla(\varphi+a_{\omega})
.

For completeness we give here a derivation of the equation for
\partial_{t}\varphi
in (
B.1
),
which slightly differs from the one in
[
21
]
.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Restricting Euler’s equations (
1.1
) to the boundary
\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}
,
using that
p=0
on the boundary, we have (
g=1
)

(B.14)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+P\cdot\nabla)P=a\nabla h,\qquad a:=-\partial_{3}p,

(B.15)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+P\cdot\nabla)B=a-1.

From these we get an evolution equations for
\nabla\varphi+\nabla a_{\omega}=P+\nabla hB
,
that is, the tangential component of the velocity field restricted to the boundary:

(B.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}(\nabla\varphi+\nabla a_{\omega})&=-P\cdot\nabla P-a\nabla h+\nabla h\big(-P\cdot\nabla B+a-1\big)+(\nabla\partial_{t}h)B\\
&=-\nabla h-P\cdot\nabla P-\nabla h(P\cdot\nabla B)+B\nabla(-\nabla h\cdot P+B),\end{split}

having used the kinematic boundary condition
\partial_{t}h=-\nabla h\cdot P+B
.
We then rewrite (
B.16
) as

(B.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}(\nabla\varphi+\nabla a_{\omega})&=\nabla\big(-h+(1/2)B^{2}-B\nabla h\cdot P-(1/2)|P|^{2}\big)\\
&+(1/2)\nabla|P|^{2}-P\cdot\nabla P-\nabla h(P\cdot\nabla B)+\nabla B(\nabla h\cdot P).\end{split}

To conclude, we observe that the last line above vanishes in view of the
condition
\omega\cdot n=0
.
In fact, using that, for
i=1,2
,
(\partial_{i}F)|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}=\partial_{i}(F|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}})-(\partial_{3}F)|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\partial_{i}h
,
we can calculate

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla\times v\cdot n&=-\partial_{1}h\big(\partial_{2}v_{3}-\partial_{3}v_{2}\big)|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}-\partial_{2}h\big(\partial_{3}v_{1}-\partial_{1}v_{3}\big)|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}+(\partial_{1}v_{2}-\partial_{2}v_{1})|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\\
&=-\partial_{1}h\big(\partial_{2}B-\partial_{3}v_{3}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\partial_{2}h-\partial_{3}v_{2}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\big)-\partial_{2}h\big(\partial_{3}v_{1}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}-\partial_{1}B+\partial_{3}v_{3}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\partial_{1}h\big)\\
&+(\partial_{1}P_{2}-\partial_{3}v_{2}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\partial_{1}h-\partial_{2}P_{1}+\partial_{3}v_{1}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\partial_{2}h)\\
&=-\partial_{1}h\partial_{2}B+\partial_{2}h\partial_{1}B+\partial_{1}P_{2}-\partial_{2}P_{1}=\curl(P+B\nabla h).\end{split}

The
\curl
above naturally denotes the scalar operator in
2
d.
Note how the same calculation with
v_{\omega},P_{\omega},B_{\omega}
instead of
v,P,B
,
shows (
B.6
) since
\nabla\times v_{\omega}\cdot n=\curl(P_{\omega}+\nabla hB_{\omega})
.
Using also vector identities, it follows that

(B.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(1/2)\nabla|P|^{2}-P\cdot\nabla P-\nabla h(P\cdot\nabla B)+\nabla B(\nabla h\cdot P)\\
&=\big(-\nabla B\cdot\nabla^{\perp}h+(\curl P)\big)P^{\perp}=0\end{split}

From (
B.17
) we have thus arrived at

(B.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}(\varphi+a_{\omega})&=-h+\frac{1}{2}B^{2}-B\nabla h\cdot P-\frac{1}{2}|P|^{2}.\end{split}

To finally obtain (
B.1
) we rewrite this as

(B.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}\varphi&=-h-\frac{1}{2}|P_{ir}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}B_{ir}^{2}-B_{ir}\nabla h\cdot P_{ir}-\partial_{t}a_{\omega}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}|P_{\omega}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}B_{\omega}^{2}-B_{\omega}\nabla h\cdot P_{\omega}-P_{ir}P_{\omega}+B_{ir}B_{\omega}-B_{ir}\nabla h\cdot P_{\omega}-B_{\omega}\nabla h\cdot P_{ir}.\end{split}

The first line of (
B.20
) matches the first three terms on the right-hand side of
the equation (
B.1
) for
\partial_{t}\varphi
(see (
B.3
))
plus the terms that contain a
\partial_{t}
(see (
B.7
)).

The desired claim then follows provide we verify that all the terms
in the second line of (
B.20
) match the remaining terms in (
B.1
), that is,
the expression

\displaystyle-\nabla\varphi\cdot P_{\omega}-\dfrac{1}{2}|P_{\omega}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(P_{\omega}\cdot\nabla h)^{2}+(G(h)\varphi)P_{\omega}\cdot\nabla h.

This can be done by direct inspection using (
B.5
) and (
B.3
)-(
B.4
).
∎

B.2.
Evolution equations for
u

Here we diagonalize (
B.1
) and derive the main boundary equations (
B.24
)
in terms of the single complex valued unknown
h+i\Lambda^{1/2}\varphi
.
(
B.24
) are the main equations at the boundary, and are used to show
decay for
u
in Section
6
.

Let us introduce some notation for the Dirichlet-Neumann map: we let

(B.21)

\displaystyle G(h)\varphi=|\nabla|\varphi+G_{\geq 2}(h)\varphi=|\nabla|\varphi+G_{2}(h)\varphi+G_{\geq 3}(h,\varphi)

(B.22)

\displaystyle G_{2}(h)\varphi:=-\nabla\cdot(h\nabla\varphi)-|\nabla|(h|\nabla|\varphi),

and
G_{\geq 3}
, defined by (
B.21
),
contains cubic and higher order terms in
(h,\varphi)
; see Proposition
B.5
below.
The following is a direct consequence of (
B.1
):

Lemma B.3
.

Let

(B.23)

\displaystyle u=h+i\Lambda^{1/2}\varphi,\qquad h=\frac{1}{2}(u+\bar{u}),\quad\varphi=\frac{1}{2i\Lambda^{1/2}}(u-\bar{u}).

Then

(B.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}u+i\Lambda^{1/2}u&=B_{0}+N_{2}+N_{3},\end{split}

where:

•

B_{0}
are the quadratic terms in
(h,\varphi)
given by

(B.25)

\displaystyle B_{0}:=-\nabla\cdot(h\nabla\varphi)-|\nabla|(h|\nabla|\varphi)+i\Lambda^{1/2}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(|\nabla|\varphi)^{2}\Big).

•

N_{2}
gathers quadratic terms with at least one rotational term:

(B.26)

\displaystyle N_{2}:=-\nabla\varphi\cdot P_{\omega}-\frac{1}{2}|P_{\omega}|^{2}-i\Lambda^{-1/2}R\cdot\partial_{t}P_{\omega}.

•

The remainders of cubic and higher homogeneity are given by

(B.27)

\displaystyle\begin{split}N_{3}:=G_{\geq 3}(h,h,\varphi)+i\Lambda^{1/2}\left[\frac{(G(h)\varphi+\nabla h\cdot\nabla\varphi)^{2}}{2(1+|\nabla h|^{2})}-\frac{1}{2}(|\nabla|\varphi)^{2}+R_{\omega}\right]\end{split}

where
R_{\omega}
given as in (
B.10
).

B.3.
Symbols of quadratic operators

By defining the symbols

(B.28)

\displaystyle q(\xi,\eta):=\frac{1}{4i|\eta|^{1/2}}\big(\xi\cdot\eta-|\xi||\eta|\big)-\frac{i|\xi|^{1/2}}{8|\eta|^{1/2}|\xi-\eta|^{1/2}}\big(\eta\cdot(\xi-\eta)+|\eta||\xi-\eta|\big)

and

(B.29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}b_{++}(\xi,\eta)&:=q(\xi,\eta),\\
b_{+-}(\xi,\eta)&:=-q(\xi,\eta)+q(\xi,\xi-\eta),\\
b_{--}(\xi,\eta)&:=-q(\xi,\eta),\end{split}

we write the quadratic terms in (
B.25
) as

(B.30)

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{0}=B_{0}(u,u)&=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in\{+,-\}}B_{\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}}(u_{\epsilon_{1}},u_{\epsilon_{2}}),\end{split}

consistently with the notation symbols/operator (
A.12
).
Note how we have expressed only the quadratic terms in
(h,\varphi)
as functions of
(u,\bar{u})

since these are the only terms on which we will need to use Fourier analysis. In particular,
(
B.24
)-(
B.27
) with (
B.29
)-(
B.30
) give us (
6.2
)-(
6.5
).

It is not hard to verify that the symbols satisfy, see (
A.13
) and Lemma
A.2
,

(B.31)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\big\|b_{\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}}^{k,k_{1},k_{2}}\big\|}_{\mathcal{S}^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{k}2^{\min(k_{1},k_{2})/2}.\end{split}

Note that we chose to write this bound by putting in evidence the vanishing in the output frequency
|\xi|

since this will be helpful in the nonlinear analysis.
Moreover, one can also directly check that the same bounds hold true after
commuting with vector fields since, see (
A.19
),

(B.32)

\displaystyle q^{S}(\xi,\eta)=-\frac{3}{2}q(\xi,\eta),\qquad q^{\Omega}(\xi,\eta)=0,

and therefore, according to the definition (
A.11
) we have, for all
j
,

(B.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\big\|(b_{\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}})^{\Gamma^{j}}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi-\eta)\varphi_{k_{2}}(\eta)\big\|}_{\mathcal{S}^{\infty}}\lesssim_{|j|}2^{k}2^{\min(k_{1},k_{2})/2}.\end{split}

B.4.
Dirichlet-Neumann map and estimates of remainder terms

To conclude this section we give estimates for the cubic and higher order remainder terms
N_{3}

defined in (
B.27
).

First, let us recall that, in view of the a priori bounds on
h

in (
2.22
) and (
2.20
), together with the (elliptic) bound (
2.37
) on
\varphi
,
we have, for all
t\in[0,T]
, a priori, that

(B.34)

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}}{\big\|u(t)\big\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1},\quad\sum_{r+k\leq N_{0}-20}{\|u(t)\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{3p_{0}}.

Moreover, in view of the (elliptic) result of Proposition
2.14

we have the following bound on
\tilde{v}_{\omega}
, for all
t\in[0,T]
:

(B.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N}{\|\widetilde{v_{\omega}}\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{\delta},\quad\sum_{r+k\leq N-1}{\|\partial_{t}\widetilde{v_{\omega}}\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{\delta}.

where
N=N_{1}+12
, see (
2.10
); see (
2.48
).

We can then show the following estimate on the cubic remainder in the equation (
B.24
):

Lemma B.4
.

Under the a priori assumptions (
2.20
)-(
2.22
), which in particular
imply (
B.34
) and (
B.35
), we have

(B.36)

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}{\|N_{3}\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-5/4}.

To prove the above Lemma we need some bounds on the Dirichlet-Neumann operator,
which we state in the next proposition.
Recall the notation (
B.21
)-(
B.22
), that is,

G(h)\varphi=|\nabla|\varphi+G_{2}(h)\varphi+G_{\geq 3}(h,\varphi)
,
and let
G_{\geq 2}(h,\varphi):=G_{2}(h)\varphi+G_{\geq 3}(h,\varphi)
.

Proposition B.5
.

Under the assumptions (
B.34
) we have the linear bounds

(B.37)

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{0}-22}{\|G(h)\varphi\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{3p_{0}},\quad\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}-2}{\|G(h)\varphi\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1+},

the quadratic bounds

(B.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{0}-24}{\|G_{\geq 2}(h,\varphi)\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-1+3p_{0}},\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}-4}{\|G_{\geq 2}(h,\varphi)\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-4/3},

and the cubic bounds

(B.39)

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{0}-26}{\|G_{\geq 3}(h,\varphi)\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{3}\langle t\rangle^{-5/4}.

The above estimate are rather standard and essentially based on a Taylor expansion for small
h
of
the Dirichlet-Neumann map. In particular, they do not require any paralinearization argument,
and losses of derivatives are allowed, as one can see from the number of vector fields that we use.
However, to our knowledge, they cannot
be found in one single reference in the exact way that they are stated above.
Without the vector fields
S
and
\Omega
these are proven, for example, in
[
19
]
;
Proposition F.1 there gives explicit bounds for the quartic and
higher remainder terms, while the term of homogeneity up to three can be handled explicitly.
In the same reference the authors also give estimates involving a weight
x
,
which resemble those for the scaling vector field
S
.
Estimates with rotation vector fields are included in the work of Deng-Ionescu-Pausader-Pusateri
[
17
]
;
see Proposition B.1 there.
Analogous estimates with the scaling vector fields can also be derived in the same exact way
5
5

5

The
scaling vector field is not included in the estimates
for the DN map in
[
17
]
since that work deals with the gravity-capillary waves system
which is not scale invariant.

In what follows, we first use Proposition
B.5

to obtain the estimate (
B.36
) on the cubic remainder
N_{3}
in the main evolution equation (
B.24
).
We will then sketch the proof of Proposition
B.5
at the end of this section,
relying on Lemma
B.6
.

Proof of Lemma
B.4
.

Looking at the definition (
B.27
) we see that the term
G_{\geq 3}

is already estimated as desired using (
B.39
).
The remaining terms are

(B.40)

\displaystyle N_{3,1}

\displaystyle:=i\Lambda^{1/2}\frac{1}{2}\left[(G(h)\varphi)^{2}-(|\nabla|\varphi)^{2}\right],

(B.41)

\displaystyle N_{3,2}

\displaystyle:=i\Lambda^{1/2}\frac{1}{2}\big((G(h)\varphi)\big)^{2}\left[\frac{1}{1+|\nabla h|^{2}}-1\right],

(B.42)

\displaystyle N_{3,3}

\displaystyle:=i\Lambda^{1/2}\frac{(G(h)\varphi)\,(\nabla h\cdot\nabla\varphi)^{2}}{(1+|\nabla h|^{2})},

(B.43)

\displaystyle N_{3,4}

\displaystyle:=-i\Lambda^{-1/2}R\cdot\partial_{t}\big(\nabla h(\nabla h\cdot P_{\omega})\big),

(B.44)

\displaystyle N_{3,5}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{i}{2}\Lambda^{1/2}(P_{\omega}\cdot\nabla h)^{2},

(B.45)

\displaystyle N_{3,6}

\displaystyle:=i\Lambda^{1/2}\big[(G(h)\varphi)P_{\omega}\cdot\nabla h\big].

The first term can be written as
2N_{3,1}=i\Lambda^{1/2}G_{\geq 2}(h,\varphi)(G(h)\varphi+|\nabla|\varphi)
,
and we can use (
A.20
)
followed by (
B.38
) and (
B.37
) and (
B.34
) to bound
(recall from (
2.9
) that
N_{1}\geq N_{0}/2+5
):

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}

\displaystyle{\|N_{3,1}\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}{\|G_{\geq 2}(h,\varphi)\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}-10}\big({\|G(h)\varphi\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}}+{\||\nabla|\varphi\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}-10}{\|G_{\geq 2}(h,\varphi)\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}}\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}\big({\|G(h)\varphi\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}+{\||\nabla|\varphi\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-3/4}\cdot\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-3/4}+\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-4/3}\cdot\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{3p_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{3}\langle t\rangle^{-5/4},

consistently with (
B.36
).

The terms (
B.41
) and (
B.42
) are easily
estimated using (
A.20
) and the linear bounds (
B.34
) and (
B.37
).

For the term (
B.43
) we first use fractional integration and the
standard commutation rules to estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}{\|N_{3,4}\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}{\|\partial_{t}\big(\nabla h(\nabla h\cdot P_{\omega})\big)\|}_{Z^{r,4/3}_{k}}

Let us look at the term where
\partial_{t}
hits the first
h
factor;
when it hits the second
h
the argument is identical, and when it hits
P_{\omega}
the estimates are even simpler.
Using product estimates, and Sobolev’s embedding, we can bound

\displaystyle\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}{\|(\partial_{t}\nabla h)(\nabla h\cdot P_{\omega})\|}_{Z^{r,4/3}_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}-10}\big({\|h\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}}+{\|\partial_{t}h\|}_{Z^{r,\infty}_{k}}\big)\big(\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}{\|h\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}+{\|\partial_{t}h\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}\big)\sum_{r+k\leq N_{1}+11}{\|P_{\omega}\|}_{Z^{r}_{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-3/4}\cdot\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{3p_{0}}\cdot\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{\delta}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-3/2},

having used (
B.34
) to estimate
h
,
(
B.37
) for
\partial_{t}h=G(h)\varphi
, (
B.35
) for
P_{\omega}
,
and
\varepsilon_{1}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1}
.

The remaining terms (
B.44
) and (
B.45
) can be estimated similarly to the ones above
using again (
B.37
), (
B.35
) and (
B.34
).
∎

The next lemma constructs and bounds the velocity potential given its boundary value at the surface.
This result is then used to obtain Proposition
B.5
.

Lemma B.6
.

Fix an integer
N\in(N_{1}+15,N_{0})\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
,
and let
N_{1}
be as above (in particular
N_{1}\geq N/2+5
).
Assume that
h
and
|\nabla|^{1/2}\varphi
satisfy the assumptions

(B.46)

\displaystyle\sum_{|r|+2|k|\leq N+1}{\|\nabla^{r}\Gamma^{k}h\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{p_{0}},\qquad\sum_{|r|+2|k|\leq N_{1}}{\|\nabla^{r}\Gamma^{k}h\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1},

and

(B.47)

\displaystyle\sum_{|r|+2|k|\leq N}{\|\nabla^{r}\Gamma^{k}|\nabla|^{1/2}\varphi\|}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{3p_{0}},\qquad\sum_{|r|+2|k|\leq N_{1}}{\|\nabla^{r}\Gamma^{k}|\nabla|^{1/2}\varphi\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1},

Then, there exists a unique solution
\psi
to the elliptic problem
\Delta\psi=0

in
\mathcal{D}_{t}
, with
\psi=\varphi
on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}
,
with
\nabla_{y}\psi\rightarrow 0
as
y\rightarrow-\infty
.
If
\Psi(x,z)=\psi(x,z+h(t,x))
, adopting the notation from
4.2
, we have the (linear)
L^{2}
bounds

(B.48)

\displaystyle{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\,\nabla_{x,z}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}+{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}|\nabla|^{1/2}\,\Psi\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{3p_{0}},\qquad n\leq N,

the (linear)
L^{\infty}_{x}
-type bounds, for all
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

(B.49)

\displaystyle{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}P_{\ell}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}+{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}|\nabla|^{1/2}P_{\ell}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1},\qquad n\leq N_{1}-1,

where
P_{\ell}
is the standard Littlewood-Paley projection in the
x
variable.

Moreover, we have the quadratic
L^{2}_{x}
-bounds

(B.50)

\displaystyle{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}(\Psi-e^{z|\nabla|}\varphi)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}+{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}|\nabla|^{1/2}(\Psi-e^{z|\nabla|}\varphi)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-1+3p_{0}},\qquad n\leq N,

and the quadratic
L^{\infty}_{x}
-bounds

(B.51)

\displaystyle{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}(\Psi-e^{z|\nabla|}\varphi)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}+{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}|\nabla|^{1/2}(\Psi-e^{z|\nabla|}\varphi)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-2+},\qquad n\leq N_{1}-1.

Note that the assumptions (
B.46
)-(
B.47
) are all
consistent with the bounds (
B.34
) for
N<N_{0}-20
.

A similar version of Lemma
B.6
is essentially contained in Appendix B of
[
17
]

(see in particular Lemma B.4).
The scaling vector field and multiple
\partial_{z}
are not included in that Lemma, but can be added
with minor changes to the proofs.
We give some details of the proof for completeness.

Proof of Lemma
B.6
.

Transforming the elliptic equation
\Delta_{x,y}\psi=0
to the flat domain as in (
C.2
) with (
C.3
)-(
C.4
)
(with the roles of
(\alpha,\beta)
played by
(\Psi,\psi)
here)
and then applying the formula (
C.17
) with
F=0
, we see that
\psi
is harmonic with (we omit the time variable)
\psi(x,h(x))=\varphi(x)
,
if and only if
\psi(x,z+h(t,x))=:\Psi(x,z)
is a fixed point of the map

(B.52)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(T\Psi)(x,z):=e^{z|\nabla|}\varphi(x)&+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}(\mathrm{sign}(z-s)E_{a}-E_{b})\,ds\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{(z+s)|\nabla|}(E_{a}-E_{b})\,ds,\end{split}

with

(B.53)

\displaystyle E_{a}(\partial\Psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nabla}{|\nabla|}\cdot\left(\nabla h\,\partial_{z}\Psi\right),\qquad E_{b}(\partial\Psi)=-|\nabla h|^{2}\partial_{z}\Psi+\nabla h\cdot\nabla\Psi.

Based on (
B.52
),
one can perform a fixed point argument in a small
C\varepsilon_{0}
ball in an apposite space (that is, the space
\mathcal{L}_{0}

in (
B.55
) below)
that will then imply the main conclusions (
B.48
)-(
B.49
),
and, as a byproduct also (
B.50
)-(
B.51
).
We define the following spaces, which will be used just within this proof:
for
g\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{p}
, for
p\in[-10,0]
, be defined by the norm

(B.54)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|g\|}_{\mathcal{F}_{p}}&:=\langle t\rangle^{-3p_{0}}\sup_{|n|\leq N+p}{\big\|\Gamma^{n}|\nabla|^{1/2}g(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}+\langle t\rangle\sup_{|n|\leq N_{1}-1+p}\sup_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\big\|\Gamma^{n}\,|\nabla|^{1/2}P_{\ell}g(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{x}};\end{split}

for
G\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times(-\infty,0]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, let
\mathcal{L}_{p}

be defined by the norm

(B.55)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|G\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{p}}&:=\langle t\rangle^{-3p_{0}}\sup_{|n|\leq N+p}\big({\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\,\nabla_{x,z}G(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}+{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}|\nabla|^{1/2}\,G(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}\big)\\
&+\langle t\rangle\sup_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sup_{|n|\leq N_{1}-1+p}\big({\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\,\nabla_{x,z}P_{\ell}G(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}+{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}|\nabla|^{1/2}\,P_{\ell}G(t)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\big).\end{split}

Note that in the
L^{\infty}_{x}
based spaces we only take the
\sup
over Littlewood-Paley projections.
These spaces are natural ones to estimate the Poisson kernel in.
Indeed, we have

(B.56)

\displaystyle{\|e^{z|\nabla|}\varphi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{p}}\lesssim{\|\varphi\|}_{\mathcal{F}_{p}};

the estimate for the
L^{2}_{x}
components follows from the bounds (
3.62
)-(
3.63
);
the estimate for the
L^{\infty}_{x}
components follow from the standard
estimates for each fixed Littlewood-Paley piece

(B.57)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\||\nabla|e^{z|\nabla|}P_{\ell}\varphi\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim{\||\nabla|^{1/2}P_{\ell}\varphi\|}_{L^{\infty}},\\
&{\||\nabla|^{1/2}e^{z|\nabla|}P_{\ell}\varphi\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim{\||\nabla|^{1/2}P_{\ell}\varphi\|}_{L^{\infty}},\qquad\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}.\end{split}

We also have bounds for bulk integrals like those appearing in (
B.52
):

(B.58)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\Big\|\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}\mathbf{1}_{\pm}(z-s)F(\cdot,s)\,ds\Big\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{p}}&\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-3p_{0}}\sup_{|n|\leq N+p}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}F(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}\\
&+\langle t\rangle\sup_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sup_{|n|\leq N_{1}-1+p}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}P_{\ell}F(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}.\end{split}

The bound (
B.58
) for the
L^{2}_{x}
-based components of the norm are implied by (
3.64
).
The bounds for the
L^{\infty}_{x}
-based components are instead obtained
from the following
L^{\infty}_{x}
-based estimates at fixed dyadic frequency

(B.59)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\Big\|\nabla_{x,z}P_{\ell}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}\mathbf{1}_{\pm}(z-s)F(\cdot,s)\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
&+{\Big\||\nabla|^{1/2}P_{\ell}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}\mathbf{1}_{\pm}(z-s)F(\cdot,s)\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim{\|F\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}},\end{split}

and then using the same argument that gives (
3.64
)
by applying vector fields and using the commutation identities (
C.28
).
See
C.2
for the details.

Applying (
B.56
) and (
B.58
) to (
B.52
) we have

(B.60)

\displaystyle{\|T\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\lesssim{\|\varphi\|}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}

\displaystyle+\langle t\rangle^{-3p_{0}}\sup_{|n|\leq N}\big({\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}E_{a}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}+{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}E_{b}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}\big)

(B.61)

\displaystyle+\langle t\rangle\sup_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sup_{|n|\leq N_{1}-1}\big({\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}P_{\ell}E_{a}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}+{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}P_{\ell}E_{b}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\big).

In view of the definition (
B.54
) and the assumption (
B.47
)
we have
{\|\varphi\|}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}
.

From the definitions of the nonlinear terms in (
B.53
),
distributing vector fields as usual, and using the assumptions on
h
in (
B.46
),
we see that

(B.62)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{|n|\leq N}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}E_{a}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim\sup_{|n|\leq N}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}\sup_{|n|\leq N/2}{\big\|\Gamma^{n}\nabla h\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}+\sup_{|n|\leq N/2}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\sup_{|n|\leq N}{\big\|\Gamma^{n}\nabla h\big\|}_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{3p_{0}}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\cdot\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1}+\langle t\rangle^{-1+}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\cdot\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{p_{0}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1+3p_{0}}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}};\end{split}

note that we have used Bernstein’s inequality to deduce the inequality for
the
L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}
norm of
\nabla_{x,z}\Psi
as follows: for
|n|\leq N/2

\displaystyle{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\sum_{\ell}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}P_{\ell}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\log(2+t)\sup_{\ell}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}P_{\ell}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\sum_{2^{\ell}\geq\langle t\rangle^{5}}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}P_{\ell}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\sum_{2^{\ell}\leq\langle t\rangle^{-5}}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\log(2+t)\langle t\rangle^{-1}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}+\langle t\rangle^{-5}\sup_{n\leq N/2+3}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}+\langle t\rangle^{-5}\sup_{n\leq N/2}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1+}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}

A bound as in (
B.62
) also holds for
E_{b}
, so that, in particular,
the nonlinear terms in (
B.60
) are bounded by
\varepsilon_{0}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
.

We can use similar argument to estimate the
L^{\infty}_{x}
components of the norm appearing in (
B.61
):
for any
|n|\leq N_{1}-1
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(B.63)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}P_{\ell}E_{a}(t)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim\sup_{n\leq N_{1}-1}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\sup_{n\leq N_{1}-1}{\big\|\Gamma^{n}\nabla h\big\|}_{L^{\infty}}\\
&\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1+}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\cdot\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-2+}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}},\end{split}

having once again used Bernstein to deduce the bound on the
L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}
norm of
\nabla_{x,z}\Psi

for very large and very small frequencies from the stronger
L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}
norm.
The same bound holds for
E_{b}
.

We have thus obtained

{\|T\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\lesssim{\|\varphi\|}_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}+\varepsilon_{0}{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
,
and in the same way we can estimate differences and obtain

{\|T(\Psi_{1}-\Psi_{2})\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}{\|\Psi_{1}-\Psi_{2}\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}.

We therefore have a unique fixed point for the map
T
, hence a unique solution to the given elliptic problem
that satisfies
{\|\Psi\|}_{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}
; in view of the definition (
B.54
),
this gives the desired (
B.48
)-(
B.49
).

To conclude, we show how (
B.50
) and (
B.51
) are implied by the bounds just proven above.
Indeed, since

(B.64)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi-e^{z|\nabla|}\varphi&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}(\mathrm{sign}(z-s)E_{a}-E_{b})\,ds\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{(z+s)|\nabla|}(E_{a}-E_{b})\,ds,\end{split}

see (
B.52
), the bounds (
3.64
) together with the estimate (
B.62
) (and the analogous one with
E_{b}
instead of
E_{a}
) imply (
B.50
),
while (
B.59
) (more precisely, its version with vector fields) together with the estimate (
B.63
) (and the analogous one with
E_{b}
instead of
E_{a}
) give (
B.51
).
∎

Remark B.7
.

The proof of (
B.49
) shows that if we replace the
L^{\infty}
bound in (
B.47
)
by a slightly stronger assumption with an
\ell^{1}
sum over frequencies, that is,

(B.65)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{|r|+|k|\leq N_{1}}{\|\nabla^{r}\Gamma^{k}|\nabla|^{1/2}P_{\ell}\varphi\|}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1},

then we can obtain the stronger conclusion

(B.66)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}\nabla_{x,z}P_{\ell}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{n}|\nabla|^{1/2}P_{\ell}\Psi\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1},\qquad n\leq N_{1}-1,

instead of (
B.49
).
Indeed, it suffices to sum over the index
\ell
in
the bounds (
B.57
) and use the stronger assumption (
B.65
),
and sum over
\ell
in the inhomogeneous bounds (
B.59
)
and estimate the sum over
\ell
of the right-hand side of (
B.62
)
using the stronger
L^{2}_{x}
bounds for very large and very small frequencies.
The estimate (
B.65
) is obtained in Section
6
,
see Remark
6.1
.
The estimate (
B.66
) is used to deduce decay for the irrotational component
of the velocity in the interior; see Lemma
2.12
.

Proof of Proposition
B.5
.

In Lemma
B.6
we gave bounds on
\Psi
following from the assumptions (
B.34
).
Since

(B.67)

\displaystyle G(h)\varphi=(1+|\nabla h|^{2})\partial_{z}\Psi|_{z=0}-\nabla h\cdot\nabla_{x}\Psi|_{z=0},

the bounds in (
B.48
) imply the first bound in (
B.37
) provided
N
is chosen large enough,
and the second bound in (
B.49
) implies the second bound in (
B.37
),
where the small
\langle t\rangle^{0+}
loss is coming from estimating the
\ell^{1}
sum over dyadic indexes by
the
\ell^{\infty}
norm for an
O(\log\langle t\rangle)
set of frequencies
2^{\ell}\in[\langle t\rangle^{-5},\langle t\rangle^{5}]
, and using
the bound on the
L^{2}_{x}
-norm in (
B.48
) for the remaining very small and very high frequencies.

To obtain the quadratic bounds (
B.38
) it suffices to observe that

\displaystyle G_{\geq 2}(h)\varphi

\displaystyle=G(h)\varphi-|\nabla|\varphi

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}\big(\Psi-e^{z|\nabla|}\varphi\big)|_{z=0}+|\nabla h|^{2}\partial_{z}\Psi|_{z=0}-\nabla h\cdot\nabla_{x}\Psi|_{z=0},

and use (
B.50
) and (
B.51
) in addition to (
B.48
)-(
B.49
).

The last estimate (
B.39
) can be obtained from similar arguments
and the fixed point formulation in the proof of Lemma
B.6
;
one needs to expand to one more order in the Taylor series for
G(h)\varphi
,
and use (
B.48
)-(
B.51
),
along the lines of the arguments in
[
17
]
.∎

Appendix C
The elliptic system for the vector potential

This appendix contains the details of the proofs of the supporting results in Section
3
.

C.1.
The elliptic equation

We first give the proof of Lemma
3.9
that derives the elliptic system in the half-space.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

This is a somewhat standard calculation but we include some details for the convenience of the reader.
The main point is to translate the boundary conditions (
3.3b
)-(
3.3c
)
to the flat domain and obtain boundary conditions for
\alpha
.

The Poisson equation (
3.42a
).

The first step is to express the Laplacian
\Delta_{x,y}
in terms of the new coordinates.
For this we compute the inverse metric

g^{-1}
in this coordinate system, whose components
g^{ab}
are given by

(C.1)

g^{ab}=\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}X^{a}\partial_{j}X^{b},

with
X^{1}(x,y)=x^{1},X^{2}(x,y)=x^{2},X^{3}(x,y)=y-h(x)
. We compute

\displaystyle g^{11}=g^{22}=1,\qquad g^{33}=1+|\nabla h|^{2},

\displaystyle g^{i3}=g^{3i}=-\partial_{i}h,\quad i=1,2,

and the remaining entries vanish.
Since
\det g=1
, the Laplacian in these coordinates takes the form

\Delta=g^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}+\partial_{a}(g^{ab})\partial_{b}.

We have

\displaystyle g^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}+(1+|\nabla h|^{2})\partial_{z}^{2},-2\partial_{1}h\partial_{1}\partial_{z}-2\partial_{2}h\partial_{2}\partial_{z},

\displaystyle\partial_{a}(g^{ab})\partial_{b}=-\partial_{1}^{2}h\partial_{z}-\partial_{2}^{2}h\partial_{z},

and adding these together we find

\Delta q=(\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}+\partial_{z}^{2})q+\partial_{z}(|\nabla h|^{2}\partial_{z}q)-\partial_{1}(\partial_{1}h\partial_{z}q)-\partial_{2}(\partial_{2}h\partial_{z}q)-\partial_{z}\left(\nabla h\cdot\nabla q\right).

In terms of
\alpha(X,z)=\beta(X,z+h(X))
and
W(X,z)=\omega(X,z+h(X))
,
the Poisson equation (
3.3a
) then reads

(C.2)

\partial_{z}^{2}\alpha+(\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2})\alpha=|\nabla|E_{a}+\partial_{z}E_{b},

where

(C.3)

\displaystyle E_{a}(\partial\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nabla}{|\nabla|}\cdot\left(\nabla h\,\partial_{z}\alpha\right)+\frac{1}{|\nabla|}W,

(C.4)

\displaystyle E_{b}(\partial\alpha)

\displaystyle=-|\nabla h|^{2}\partial_{z}\alpha+\nabla h\cdot\nabla\alpha,

where now
\nabla=\nabla_{X}
.

The boundary conditions (
3.42b
)-(
3.42d
).

We now write the boundary conditions (
3.3b
)-(
3.3c
) explicitly.
The normal vector to the boundary is

(C.5)

n=(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}(\partial_{y}-\nabla h\cdot\nabla),

which is defined for all
(x,y)
. Recalling that
\Pi_{i}^{j}=\delta_{i}^{j}-n_{i}n^{j}
,
we compute

(C.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Pi_{1}^{1}&=1-n_{1}n^{1}=1-(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}(\partial_{1}h)^{2},\\
\Pi_{1}^{2}&=-n_{1}n^{2}=-(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\partial_{1}h\partial_{2}h,\\
\Pi_{1}^{3}&=-n_{1}n^{3}=(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\partial_{1}h,\\
\Pi_{2}^{2}&=1-n_{2}n^{2}=1-(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}(\partial_{2}h)^{2},\\
\Pi_{2}^{3}&=-n_{2}n^{3}=(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\partial_{2}h,\\
\Pi_{3}^{3}&=1-n_{3}n^{3}=1-(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1},\end{split}

which determine the remaining components since
\Pi
is symmetric.
The boundary conditions (
3.3b
) then give us, for
i=1,2
,

(C.7)

\displaystyle\beta_{i}-(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\partial_{i}h\big(\partial_{1}h\,\beta_{1}+\partial_{2}h\beta_{2}-\beta_{3})=0

and, therefore, in terms of
\alpha
they read

(C.8)

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle=B_{i},\qquad B_{i}:=-(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\partial_{i}h(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot\alpha)|_{z=0},\quad i=1,2

as claimed in (
3.42b
)-(
3.42c
).

We now write (
3.3c
) explicitly.
We start from (
3.5
) which we rewrite as

(C.9)

\partial_{n}\beta_{n}+(\Pi_{i}^{j}\partial_{j}n^{i})\beta_{n}=0,

where we recall that
\beta_{n}=n\cdot\beta=(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2})(\beta_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}))
.
We then pass to the new coordinates using the expression (
C.5
) for the normal vector,
and calculate the first term in (
C.9
):

(C.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{n}\beta_{n}=\\
&=(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\left((1+|\nabla h|^{2})\partial_{z}-\nabla h\cdot\nabla\right)\left((1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))\right)\\
&=\partial_{z}\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot\partial_{z}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})-(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\nabla h\cdot\nabla(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))\\
&-\big((1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\nabla h\cdot\nabla(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\big)(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})),\end{split}

where the expressions above are evaluated at
z=0
.
We then write out explicitly the curvature terms
\Pi_{i}^{j}\partial_{i}n^{j}
;
we first record that

\displaystyle\partial_{i}n^{j}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{i}\left((1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\partial_{j}h\right),\quad i,j=1,2

\displaystyle\partial_{i}n^{3}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\left((1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\right),\quad i=1,2,

and then, using the expressions (
C.6
) for the projection
\Pi
, we find
the classical formula

(C.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Pi_{i}^{j}\partial_{i}n^{j}&=-\nabla\cdot\big((1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\nabla h\big).\end{split}

In view of (
C.10
) and (
C.11
), the boundary condition (
C.9
) then reads

(C.12)

\partial_{z}\alpha_{3}=B_{3},

where

(C.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{3}&=\nabla h\cdot\partial_{z}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})+(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\nabla h\cdot\nabla(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))\\
&+\big((1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\nabla h\cdot\nabla(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\big)(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))\\
&+\big[\nabla\cdot\big((1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\nabla h\big)\big](1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1/2}\big(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\big)\\
&=\nabla h\cdot\partial_{z}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})+(1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\nabla h\cdot\nabla(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))\\
&+A(\nabla h,\nabla^{2}h)(\alpha_{3}-\nabla h\cdot(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}))\end{split}

with

(C.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A(\nabla h,\nabla^{2}h):=\nabla\cdot\big((1+|\nabla h|^{2})^{-1}\nabla h\big).\end{split}

This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.9
.
∎

Next, we give the formulas for the solution of Poisson’s equation in the half-space
that are used to obtain the fixed point formulation of Lemma
3.10
.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
u:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{x}\times\{z\leq 0\}
be the solution of

(C.15)

\displaystyle(\partial_{z}^{2}+\Delta_{x})u=\partial_{z}E_{a}+|\nabla|E_{b}+F,\quad\text{ in}\quad{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{x}\times\{z<0\}.

Then:

(i)

If we assign Dirichlet boundary conditions

(C.16)

\displaystyle u(x,0)

\displaystyle=B(x)

u
is formally given by

(C.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}u(x,z)=e^{z|\nabla|}B(x)&+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}\Big(\mathrm{sign}(z-s)E_{a}-E_{b}-\frac{1}{|\nabla|}F\Big)\,ds\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{(z+s)|\nabla|}\Big(E_{a}-E_{b}-\frac{1}{|\nabla|}F\Big)\,ds.\end{split}

(ii)

If we assign Neumann boundary conditions

(C.18)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}u(x,0)

\displaystyle=B^{\prime}(x)

u
is formally given by

(C.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}u(x,z)&=\frac{1}{|\nabla|}e^{z|\nabla|}B^{\prime}(x)-\frac{1}{|\nabla|}e^{z|\nabla|}E_{a}(z=0)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{(z+s)|\nabla|}\big(-E_{a}+E_{b}+\frac{1}{|\nabla|}F\big)\,ds\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}\big(\mathrm{sign}(z-s)E_{a}-E_{b}-\frac{1}{|\nabla|}F\big)\,ds.\end{split}

Proof.

Taking the Fourier transform in
x

one obtains the general solution of
(\partial_{z}^{2}+\Delta_{x})u=f
in the lower half-space,
that decays to zero as
z\rightarrow-\infty
, in the form

(C.20)

\displaystyle\widehat{u}(\xi,z)=c_{1}e^{z|\xi|}+\int_{-\infty}^{z}\frac{1}{2|\xi|}\big(e^{(z-s)|\xi|}-e^{(s-z)|\xi|}\big)\widehat{f}(s,\xi)\,ds.

Imposing the boundary condition (
C.16
) gives

\widehat{u}(z,\xi)=\widehat{u}(0,\xi)e^{z|\xi|}+\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{2|\xi|}e^{(z+s)|\xi|}\widehat{f}\,ds-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{2|\xi|}e^{-|z-s||\xi|}\widehat{f}\,ds.

When
f
is the right-hand of (
C.15
), an integration by parts in
s
on the terms
\partial_{s}E_{a}

gives the formula (
C.17
).

When instead we impose Neumann boundary conditions (
C.18
), from (
C.20
) we compute

\displaystyle\widehat{B^{\prime}}(\xi)=c_{1}|\xi|+\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{2}\big(e^{-s|\xi|}+e^{s|\xi|}\big)\hat{f}(s,\xi)\,ds,

and therefore

\displaystyle\widehat{u}(\xi,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\xi|}e^{z|\xi|}\Big(\widehat{B^{\prime}}(\xi)-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{2}\big(e^{-s|\xi|}+e^{s|\xi|}\big)\hat{f}(s,\xi)\,ds\Big)

\displaystyle+\int_{-\infty}^{z}\frac{1}{2|\xi|}\big(e^{(z-s)|\xi|}-e^{(s-z)|\xi|}\big)\hat{f}(s,\xi)\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\xi|}e^{z|\xi|}\widehat{B^{\prime}}(\xi)-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{2|\xi|}e^{(z+s)|\xi|}\hat{f}(s,\xi)\,ds-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{2|\xi|}e^{-|z-s||\xi|}\hat{f}(s,\xi)\,ds.

Plugging-in for
f
the right-hand side of (
C.15
) and integrating by parts on the
\partial_{s}E_{a}
term,
we obtain (
C.19
).
∎

C.2.
Bounds for the Poisson kernel in weighted spaces

In this subsection we give the proof of Lemma
3.13
.

Proof of Lemma
3.13
.

We prove the estimates (
3.62
)-(
3.64
) as well as
L^{\infty}_{x}

based estimates that are used in the proof of Lemma
B.6
.

Proof of (
3.62
) and (
3.63
)
.
We being by recalling the following standard bounds for the Poisson Kernel,

(C.21)

\displaystyle{\big\|e^{z|\nabla|}f\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{p}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\|f\|}_{L^{p}_{x}},\qquad 1<p<\infty,

(C.22)

\displaystyle{\big\||\nabla|^{1/2}e^{z|\nabla|}f\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\|f\|}_{L^{2}_{x}}.

The estimates (
C.21
)-(
C.22
) give (
3.62
) and (
3.63
) with
r=0=k
.
The bounds with
k=0
and any
r
follow immediately.
To prove the bound with vector fields we first compute the commutator of
\underline{\Gamma}=\Omega
or

\underline{S}=z\partial_{z}+x\cdot\nabla+(1/2)t\partial_{t}
with the Poisson kernel:

(C.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\underline{\Gamma},e^{z|\nabla|}]&=c_{\Gamma}e^{z|\nabla|},\qquad c_{\underline{S}}=-2,\quad c_{\Omega}=0,\\
[\underline{\Gamma},|\nabla|^{\ell}]&=d_{\Gamma,\ell}|\nabla|^{\ell},\qquad d_{\underline{S},\ell}=\ell,\quad d_{\Omega,\ell}=0,\end{split}

which are easy to see, for example taking the Fourier transform in
x
.

Then, using (
C.23
) and (
C.21
),
and recalling the definition of the
Z^{r,p}_{k}
spaces, we have

\displaystyle{\big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{k}e^{z|\nabla|}f\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}W^{r,p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq k}{\big\|e^{z|\nabla|}\underline{\Gamma}^{k^{\prime}}f\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}W^{r,p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq k}{\big\|\Gamma^{k^{\prime}}f\big\|}_{W^{r,p}}={\|f\|}_{Z^{r,p}_{k}}.

This is (
3.62
).
The second estimate (
3.63
) can be obtained in the same way.

Proof of (
3.64
)
.
We adopt the short-hand

\displaystyle T_{\pm}f(x,z):=\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}\mathbf{1}_{\pm}(s-z)f(x,s)\,ds.

Consider first the case without vector fields,
k=0
.
In what follows, we let
p\in[1,2]
and
q\in[2,\infty]
.
Taking the Fourier transform in
x
we have

(C.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big(T_{\pm}f(x,z)\big)=e^{-|\cdot||\xi|}\mathbf{1}_{\mp}(\cdot)\ast_{z}\widehat{f}(\xi,\cdot)\mathbf{1}_{-}(\cdot).

Using the Littlewood-Paley projectors
P_{l}
,
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(see (
2.57
)),
and orthogonality we see that

\displaystyle{\|T_{\pm}f(x,z)\|}_{L^{q}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}\approx{\big\|{\|\mathcal{F}\big(T_{\pm}P_{l}f(\cdot,z)\big)\|}_{\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})L^{2}_{\xi}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\big\|}_{L^{q}_{z}}.

Applying Minkowski’s inequality, followed by (
C.24
) and Youngs’s inequality with
1+1/q=1/p+1/\rho
,
gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\|T_{\pm}f(x,z)\|}_{L^{q}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}&\lesssim{\big\|{\|\varphi_{[l-2,l+2]}(\xi)e^{-|\cdot||\xi|}\mathbf{1}_{\mp}(\cdot)\ast_{z}\varphi_{l}(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi,\cdot)\|}_{L^{q}_{z}}\big\|}_{\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})L^{2}_{\xi}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\\
&\lesssim{\big\|{\|\varphi_{[l-2,l+2]}(\xi)e^{-|\cdot||\xi|}\|}_{L^{\rho}_{z}}\,{\|\varphi_{l}(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi,\cdot)\|}_{L^{p}_{z}}\big\|}_{\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})L^{2}_{\xi}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\\
&\lesssim{\big\|{\|2^{-l/\rho}\varphi_{l}(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi,\cdot)\|}_{L^{p}_{z}}\big\|}_{\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})L^{2}_{\xi}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}.\end{split}

Applying again Minkowski and using orthogonality we get

(C.25)

\displaystyle{\big\||\nabla|^{(1+1/q-1/p)}T_{\pm}f(x,z)\big\|}_{L^{q}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\|f\|}_{L^{p}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}.

Using
(q,p)=(\infty,2)
and
(2,2)
we obtain the bounds

(C.26)

\displaystyle{\big\||\nabla|^{1/2}T_{\pm}f(x,z)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}+{\big\||\nabla|T_{\pm}f(x,z)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\|f\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}.

Similar estimates hold if we replace the kernel
e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}
with
e^{(z+s)|\nabla|}
;
see also Remark
3.14
.
Using instead (
C.25
) with
(q,p)=(\infty,6/5)
and
(2,6/5)
we obtain the bounds

(C.27)

\displaystyle{\big\||\nabla|^{1/2}T_{\pm}f(x,z)\big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}+{\big\||\nabla|T_{\pm}f(x,z)\big\|}_{L^{2}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\||\nabla|^{1/3}f\|}_{L^{6/5}_{z}L^{2}_{x}}.

The bounds (
C.26
) and (
C.27
) give us (
3.64
) with
k=0
and any
r
.

To obtain the estimates with vector fields it suffices to use the following
identities:

(C.28)

\displaystyle\underline{\Gamma}T_{\pm}

\displaystyle=T_{\pm}\underline{\Gamma}f+c^{\pm}_{\underline{\Gamma}}T_{\pm}f,

where
c^{\pm}_{\underline{S}}=-1
and
c^{\pm}_{\Omega}=0
.
The identity for
\underline{\Gamma}=\Omega
is obvious.
To obtain the one for
\underline{\Gamma}=\underline{S}
, recall (
2.4
),
it suffices to show the same identity just for the spatial part
\underline{\Sigma}:=z\partial_{z}+x\cdot\nabla_{x}
.
Observe that for any
\tau

[x\cdot\nabla_{x},e^{\tau|\nabla|}]=-(2+\tau|\nabla|)e^{\tau|\nabla|}

and, therefore,

(C.29)

[z\partial_{z}+x\cdot\nabla_{x},e^{(z-s)|\nabla|}]=(s|\nabla|-2)e^{(z-s)|\nabla|}=(-s\partial_{s}-2)e^{(z-s)|\nabla|}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\underline{\Sigma}T_{+}f(x,z)&=\underline{\Sigma}\int_{z}^{0}e^{(z-s)|\nabla|}f(x,s)\,ds,\\
&=\int_{z}^{0}e^{z|\nabla|}(-s\partial_{s}e^{-s|\nabla|})f(x,s)\,ds+\int_{z}^{0}e^{(z-s)|\nabla|}(x\cdot\nabla-2)f(x,s)\,ds-zf(x,z).\end{split}

Integrating by parts in
s
in the first integral above, we see that all the boundary terms cancel out
and we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\underline{\Sigma}T_{+}f(x,z)&=\int_{z}^{0}e^{(z-s)|\nabla|}\partial_{s}\big(sf(x,s)\big)\,ds+\int_{z}^{0}e^{(z-s)|\nabla|}(x\cdot\nabla-2)f(x,s)\,ds\\
&=T_{+}\big((\underline{\Sigma}-1)f\big)(x,z),\end{split}

which implies (
C.28
) for
T_{+}
. For the operator
T_{-}
we use the same argument:
from (
C.29
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\underline{\Sigma}T_{-}f(x,z)&=\underline{\Sigma}\int_{-\infty}^{z}e^{(s-z)|\nabla|}f(x,s)\,ds,\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{z}e^{-z|\nabla|}(-s\partial_{s}e^{s|\nabla|})f(x,s)\,ds+\int_{-\infty}^{z}e^{(s-z)|\nabla|}(x\cdot\nabla-2)f(x,s)\,ds+zf(x,z)\\
&=T_{-}\big((\underline{\Sigma}-1)f\big)(x,z)\end{split}

having used again integration by parts in
s
in the last step.

To conclude we use the above commutation identities (
C.28
) and (
C.26
)-(
C.27
) to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{|k^{\prime}|\leq k}{\Big\|\underline{\Gamma}^{k^{\prime}}|\nabla|^{1/2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}\mathbf{1}_{\pm}(s-z)f(x,s)\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}H^{r}}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{|k^{\prime}|\leq k}{\Big\||\nabla|^{1/2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{-|z-s||\nabla|}\mathbf{1}_{\pm}(s-z)\,\underline{\Gamma}^{k^{\prime}}f(x,s)\,ds\Big\|}_{L^{\infty}_{z}H^{r}}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{|k^{\prime}|\leq k}\min\big({\|\Gamma^{k^{\prime}}f\|}_{L^{2}_{z}H^{r}},{\big\||\nabla|^{1/3}\Gamma^{k^{\prime}}f\big\|}_{L^{6/5}_{z}H^{r}}\big)\end{split}

This gives us the desired bound on the first term on the left-hand side of (
3.64
).
The same argument can be applied to the second term on the left-hand side of (
3.64
)
using the
L^{2}_{z}
bounds in (
C.26
) and (
C.27
).
This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.13
.
∎

Appendix D
Energy estimates: proof of Proposition
2.5

To get estimates with vector fields for solutions of (
1.1
), as opposed to estimates
that only involve derivatives
\nabla_{x,y}
, one must exploit the invariances and structure of the equations.
This structure is more transparent in the irrotational problem,
once it has been properly rewritten on the boundary,
because it is straightforward to commute the linearized operator
\partial_{t}+i\Lambda^{1/2}
with the fields

(S,\Omega)
.
For the rotational problem, we must instead commute suitable vector fields with the
full system (
1.1
).
In what follows we will
derive the higher-order system obtained by doing this;
see (
D.4
) below.
We can then verify that the commuted system (
D.4
) has essentially the same structure as the
original problem
up to acceptable lower order error terms.
For the commuted system (
D.4
)
one can then prove energy estimates following any of the available approaches,
e.g.
[
10
,
11
,
36
]
.

This system (
D.4
) is obtained by commuting
(
1.1
) with the fields
\underline{S}
and
\Omega
in (
2.4
).
More precisely, we
apply the scaling field
\underline{S}
componentwise but
use Lie derivatives
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}
(
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}X=[\Omega,X]

for vector fields
X
and
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}q=\Omega q
for functions
q
)
with respect to the rotation fields.
This is because these operators preserve the divergence-free condition

(D.1)

\displaystyle\div\underline{S}^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{n_{2}}v=0.

Moreover, the Lie derivatives
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}
commute with gradients,

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{n}\nabla_{x,y}q=\nabla_{x,y}\Omega^{n}q
, while the scaling
field satisfies

\underline{S}^{n}\nabla_{x,y}q=\nabla_{x,y}(\underline{S}-1)^{n}q
.
As a result we have the identity

(D.2)

\underline{S}^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{n_{2}}\nabla_{x,y}q=\nabla_{x,y}(\underline{S}-1)^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{n_{2}}q.

In light of the above, and the fact that
\underline{S}^{n}\partial_{t}=\partial_{t}(\underline{S}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n}
,
it is natural to work with

(D.3)

v^{n_{1},n_{2}}=\underline{S}_{1/2}^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{n_{2}}v,\quad h^{n_{1},n_{2}}=S_{1}^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}h,\quad P^{n_{1},n_{2}}=\underline{S}_{1}^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}(p+y)=\underline{S}_{1}^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}p,

where we are abbreviating
\underline{S}_{a}:=\underline{S}-a
, and similarly with
S_{a}
,
and where the last identity in (
D.3
) holds if
n_{1}+n_{2}\geq 1
.
Notice that Sobolev norms of
(v^{n_{1},n_{2}},h^{n_{1},n_{2}})

are equivalent to norms of

(\underline{S}^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}v,S^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}h)
, for
n_{1}+n_{2}\leq k
with a fixed
k
.

Our main claim is that the above variables satisfy the system

(D.4a)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla)v^{n_{1},n_{2}}+\nabla_{x,y}P^{n_{1},n_{2}}

\displaystyle=F_{n_{1},n_{2}},

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ in }\mathcal{D}_{t},

(D.4b)

\displaystyle\div v^{n_{1},n_{2}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ in }\mathcal{D}_{t},

(D.4c)

\displaystyle P^{n_{1},n_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-\partial_{y}p)h^{n_{1},n_{2}}+G_{n_{1},n_{2}},

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D}_{t},

(D.4d)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla)h^{n_{1},n_{2}}

\displaystyle=(v^{n_{1},n_{2}})\cdot(1,-\nabla h)+H_{n_{1},n_{2}},

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D}_{t},

where the terms
F_{n_{1},n_{2}},G_{n_{1},n_{2}},H_{n_{1},n_{2}}
consist of nonlinear
acceptable error terms which either (a)
involve fewer than
n_{1}
scaling or
n_{2}
rotation vector fields,
or (b) contain the highest number of vector fields but are of cubic or higher
of homogeneity in the unknowns.
We now verify this claim.
The equations (
D.4a
)-(
D.4b
) follow
after applying
\underline{S}^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{n_{2}}

to (
1.1a
)-(
1.1b
) using the identities (
D.2
), (
D.1
)
and distributing vector fields.

To derive the boundary conditions (
D.4c
)-(
D.4d
)
we decompose the fields
\underline{S},\Omega
into tangential (to the boundary)
and transverse components, by defining

\underline{S}_{T}:=\underline{S}+\underline{S}(h-y)\partial_{y},\qquad\Omega_{T}:=\Omega+\Omega(h-y)\partial_{y},

which are tangent to the boundary since they annihilate the boundary-defining
function
y-h
. Using that
\underline{S}(h-y)=(S-1)h
and
p=0

at the boundary, we find

(D.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}P^{n_{1},n_{2}}=(\underline{S}_{T}-1)^{n_{1}}\Omega_{T}^{n_{2}}p-(S-1)^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}h\partial_{y}p+G_{n_{1},n_{2}}\\
=(-\partial_{y}p)h^{n_{1},n_{2}}+G_{n_{1},n_{2}},\end{split}

where
G_{n_{1},n_{2}}
collects nonlinear error terms
where at least one of the factors of
\underline{S},\Omega
falling on
h
or
p
have been replaced with a gradient.

For (
D.4d
) we write

(\underline{S}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{n_{2}}v=(\underline{S}_{T}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{T}}^{n_{2}}v

up to nonlinear error terms with fewer vector fields, and then write, with

N:=(1,-\nabla h)
,

(D.6)

\big((\underline{S}_{T}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{T}}^{n_{2}}v\big)\cdot N=(\underline{S}_{T}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\Omega_{T}^{n_{2}}\left(v\cdot N\right)+v\cdot(\underline{S}_{T}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{n_{2}}\nabla h+F^{n_{1},n_{2}}_{3},

where
F^{n_{1},n_{2}}_{3}
collects acceptable nonlinear error terms. Note that we have included
the second term on the right-hand side of (
D.6
) since it is above the highest order;
however, it is not hard to see that this term has a favorable structure
when multiplied by
h^{n_{1},n_{2}}
and integrated in space.
Since the boundary
condition (
1.1d
) gives
v\cdot N=\partial_{t}h
and the operators

\underline{S}_{T},\Omega_{T}
are tangent to the boundary, at the boundary we have

(D.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\underline{S}_{T}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\Omega_{T}^{n_{2}}\left(v\cdot N\right)&=(\underline{S}_{T}-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\Omega_{T}^{n_{2}}\partial_{t}h=(S-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}\partial_{t}h\\
&=\partial_{t}\left((S-1)^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}h\right)\end{split}

where we used
(S-\tfrac{1}{2})\partial_{t}=\partial_{t}(S-1)
. This
yields (
D.4d
) after commuting
\nabla
with
(S-1)^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}

and then writing

v\cdot\nabla(S-\tfrac{1}{2})^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}h=v\cdot\nabla(S-1)^{n_{1}}\Omega^{n_{2}}h
up to lower-order quadratic terms.
This completes the derivation of (
D.4
).

Starting from (
D.4
), one can begin to carry out energy estimates by
(applying derivatives as in the standard case and) multiplying
the equation (
D.4a
) with (derivatives of)
v^{n_{1},n_{2}}
and integrating over
\mathcal{D}_{t}
.
Integrating by parts the pressure term one sees that bulk terms vanish
in view of (
D.4b
) and (
D.1
).
The remaining boundary integral of
P^{n_{1},n_{2}}(v^{n_{1},n_{2}}\cdot n)
,
where
n
is the unit normal,
can be manipulated using the formulas (
D.4c
) and (
D.6
)-(
D.7
).
This naturally leads to defining high order energies of the form

\displaystyle E_{n_{1},n_{2}}(t):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\big|v^{n_{1},n_{2}}\big|^{2}\,dxdy+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big(-\partial_{y}p)\big|h^{n_{1},n_{2}}\big|^{2}\,dx.

Note that the Taylor sign condition
-\partial_{y}p\geq c_{0}>0
holds automatically in our setting
of small solutions,
since (
1.1a
) gives
-\partial_{y}p=g+\partial_{t}v_{3}+v\cdot\nabla v_{3}\geq g-C\varepsilon_{0}
.
Considering these functionals with
n_{1}+n_{2}\leq k
, also including
r
regular derivatives,
one obtains the energy functionals
\mathcal{E}_{r,k}
appearing in Proposition
2.5
.
As already mentioned, the claimed a priori estimates (
2.30
) can
then be obtained following the arguments in
[
10
,
36
]
.
